Medical documentation has really evolved over the last 50 years. Back in those days, the documentation that physicians and healthcare providers did was merely to remind them about exactly what it was that they were doing with their patient. That's changed. Now, it is pretty much a document that's used for many things, specifically billing. Unless you're actually documenting what you're doing in the note for the patient, you're not going to be able to bill for that procedure or that time with the patient, and therefore you're not going to get paid. Medical documentation has really been beefed up in the last few decades to be able to justify the type of reimbursement that healthcare providers need to get, and they need to actually show that they're doing what they're doing in billing. That has to take place in medical documentation. In addition to that, it's also a legal document. It's actually showing what you're actually thinking and the reason why you're doing things. As a result of this, whereas the practice of medicine 50 years ago was very heavy on the practice and the actual doing of medicine and was pretty light on the documentation, that's changed. Now, medical documentation is starting to take up and has taken up a huge portion of the actual job of being a healthcare provider. For some, this paperwork and documentation has really caused a lot of burnout. People go into medicine, I can speak for myself, because we want to actually treat patients and be with patients and make these decisions and be at the bedside. When half your day is actually being taken up with documentation, burnout rates are high. I've seen studies that go back over a decade ago where healthcare providers were given scribes and people that would shadow them and actually do the documentation for them. Those studies showed and reported that these healthcare providers had almost like a rebirth, a re-experience of their profession and burnout rates would actually plummet. The issue was it was very expensive to actually hire an individual human being to follow you around and to write down all of the stuff that was legitimate and needed to be included in documentation.  cram.com. Welcome to another MedCram video. Today we're going to talk about CRISPR technology. This is actually gene editing technology that has tremendous potential to alleviate diseases that are rooted in genetics. Also, for the first time, CRISPR technology is now being utilized outside of the United States in the kingdom of Bahrain. That's a first. But before we get to that, I'm going to introduce myself. I'm Dr. Roger Schwelt, co-founder of MedCram.com, where we have multiple videos for continuing medical education, continuing education credits for health care providers, or for those that want to know more about their own body, test results, things of that nature. We have videos on CBC, EKG, urinalysis, lipid profiles, all sorts of tests that you might see on my chart. Join us at MedCram.com. So in late 2023, the FDA approved the first gene therapies to treat patients with sickle cell disease. This is one of the first genetic diseases that we learn about in medical school because it affects so many people, especially in the Middle East and Africa, also with thalassemia, which is often seen in patients in the Middle East as well. But this is very interesting gene editing technology, and it allows us to essentially make patients express the correct type of gene or alternative gene compared to the one that is there that is not being expressed. And this is really powerful stuff. And this is how it works. It was actually discovered in bacteria. If you can imagine it, you've got the bacteria and you've got viruses and viruses which land on the surface and they inject a foreign DNA material. This defense mechanism existed in bacteria where a protein and two RNA molecules would associate. And one of the RNA molecules was complementary to this viral DNA sequence, and it would bind to this sequence and it would actually cut the viral DNA so that it could not do what it was supposed to do. So we adapted this mechanism, and instead of viral DNA, we're able to do it with human DNA, specifically in the nucleus. So if you have a target DNA sequence that is identified, this is the part here where the gene may not be working in terms of sickle cell, et cetera, and you get a matching RNA strand that will go into this area and be very specific for just this area. This area is called the PAM. Then you get a Cas9 enzyme that also uses this RNA that binds to this particular area of the DNA, and you get a cut or a snip, basically. And when you have that snip, there's a number of things that can happen. So essentially what you've done is you've used machinery that we've developed from defense mechanisms in bacteria to be able to cut at will particular areas of DNA. And of course, there's three potential things that you can do with that. You can allow those cuts to fill back in again, and they usually do so with some sort of mutation, which can sometimes make the gene not work, which is kind of the opposite that you wanna do in medicine. So this is used more in research. We don't use that typically here. Or you can delete that, cut it out, and have it reconnect with itself again. Or what we're actually seeing now in therapeutics is to insert a strand of DNA or a gene or something that's going to do something into this area here, and then you have a new aspect of the DNA with a functioning sequence that actually does something.  cram.com. So COPD is a range of specific diseases, emphysema, chronic bronchitis. Technically, it also includes asthma and bronchiectasis, depending on how you define it. And number two, you have decrease surface area. And number three, you have secretion. And now part of that is inflammation. And today we're going to talk about how this can actually be alleviated through a new medication that's just been FDA-approved. We haven't had a new medication in the management of COPD in over a decade. So the definition of COPD is that if you're unknown to unknown unknown unknown unknown unknown unknown to talk about how this can actually be alleviated through a new medication that's just been FDA approved. We haven't had a new medication in the management of COPD in over a decade. So the definition of COPD is a FEV1 divided by FEC of less than .7. Let me unpack that for you. So there is something called spirometry, which is part of a pulmonary function test. And during that procedure, you're asked to take a deep breath in and then to blow it out as hard as you possibly can. All of the air that comes out in an unlimited amount of time is known as the forest vital capacity. That's all of the air that you can blow out. And at the same time, what they're doing is they're measuring how much air you can blow out in one second. That's what that little one means here on the FEV1. The question is, is can you blow out 70% of the air that you can completely blow out in the first second? And if the answer is yes, or you can blow more than 70%, then you don't have COPD. On the other hand, if you are so obstructed that you cannot blow more than 70% of the air in your lungs out in the first second, then you have COPD by definition. And we can classify how severe that COPD is based on the percent predicted of that FEV1 for your type of lung size. So if it's between 80% and 100% of predicted, it's stage one. If it's 50% to 79% of predicted, it's stage two. If it's 30% to 49% of predicted, it's stage three. And then finally, if it's less than 30%, it's stage four. And so we call this mild, moderate, severe, and very severe. And as you can imagine, the treatment for this type of thing is to take the bronchial, which is small, and to make it larger. That's one of the major ways that we can get air out faster. Something that's key to this is understanding that in the wall of the bronchi are little filaments of smooth muscle. And when those smooth muscles contract, it gets smaller like this. And so really what we want to do is we want to have relaxation. By the way, this is exactly the same situation that we see in asthma, except in asthma, it's reversible, completely reversible. Whereas with COPD, we don't see that reversibility. We see permanent destruction. But there is still the ability to relax these fibers enough so that we can get it back to that nice and open state. And so there's a number of inhalers that we look at. And specifically in COPD, the first inhaler that we would put on is something called a long-acting muscarinic antagonist. That's because muscarinic receptors on the smooth muscle will cause the smooth muscle to contract. And so we want to block those. So those are long-acting muscarinic antagonists. But there's also beta receptors on those smooth muscle cells. And the long-acting beta agonists are going to excite those receptors and again cause relaxation. Finally, we can sometimes use inhaled corticosteroids, which reduce inflammation. But those are the last ones that we would put on a patient with COPD, as opposed to generally going in the other direction for asthma, where we tend to start patients on inhaled corticosteroids and lab as first and then add lambas last. And for more information, if you want to learn more about COPD or just about any disease like asthma, congestive heart failure, kidney disease, liver disease, we have videos on MedCram.com that also get CME accreditation. You can learn more about this at our website MedCram.com. Let's talk about this new medication that's come out called encefen-trin. And it's a nebulized medication that patients take twice a day, three milligrams twice a day. The FDA approval was based on a couple of randomized placebo-controlled trials. But first let's talk about the mechanism, how encefen-trin works, because it works through a different mechanism than what we just talked about in terms of these inhalers. So there's two areas that are affected here by encefen-trin. There are the bronchial smooth muscle walls and there are inflammatory cells. Let's talk about the bronchial smooth muscle cells first. Inside is a second messenger system that is able to transmit the binding of chemicals on the outside of the surface of the cell and cause changes on the inside. A major enzyme that is involved with this is something called adenyl cyclase. And what this enzyme AC does is it takes something called adenyl monophosphate or AMP and it converts it into something called cyclic AMP. Cyclic AMP is a second messenger that affects change on the inside of the cell. And what it does when it goes up in concentration, it actually can affect the contraction of the smooth muscle cells. And in fact what it does is it blocks it. What we want to have is high levels of cyclic AMP. If there's an enzyme to make cyclic AMP, there's also an enzyme to break it down. And that's something called phosphodiesterase 3. Phosphodiesterase 3 causes cyclic AMP levels to drop and that can cause contraction. If we take the medication encefen-trin, what actually happens here is that encefen-trin goes into these cells and it blocks phosphodiesterase inhibitor 3. And so what that does is it tends to increase the levels of cyclic AMP and therefore prevents contraction of smooth muscles. So what you have here is you have a bronchus which is going to become larger. And what that's going to do is it's going to increase your FEV1. That's the first effect that encefen-trin does. What does it do in terms of inflammatory cells? So again, we have AC in these inflammatory cells and they take AMP once again and convert it into cyclic AMP. In this case though, cyclic AMP causes inflammation and it does it because it increases the time of survival of these inflammatory cells and it causes the increase in their production of things that cause inflammation. And encefen-trin is going to hit not only the PDE3 inhibitor in our bronchial cells but also the PDE4 inhibitor. And as a result, because we know that PDE4 causes the prevention and breakdown from cyclic AMP to cyclic AMP, what we see here is that encefen-trin is going to block PDE4 and as a result of that, it's going to increase the concentration of cyclic AMP which is going to block production of inflammation. And so actually what we have here in this situation is we have a reduction in inflammation. Just to do this again, in the bronchial smooth muscle cells, there is an enzyme called phosphodiesterase 3 and in the inflammatory cells there is an enzyme called phosphodiesterase 4. Both of these enzymes are responsible for breaking down cyclic AMP but we want cyclic AMP in both of those cells. And so what we do is we inhibit both of those enzymes that break down cyclic AMP and as a result we have an increase in both those types of cells and both of those types of cells, cyclic AMP, is going to respectively reduce contraction and it's also going to reduce inflammation. So what we get is an increase in FEV1 and a reduction in inflammation. That's what happens at the cellular level. Let's see how that actually translates into randomized placebo-controlled trials. Two randomized controlled trials, one was enhanced one, enhanced two, there were about 700 patients in both of those and what they found in terms of endpoints and what they were looking for in the endpoint was the change in FEV1 as we've already talked about. If we look at these patients, many of them, the majority in fact, were already on lambas and labas as we've already talked about are beneficial. So this is good add-on therapy. What they found was that there was an 87 milliliter improvement in enhanced one and a 94 milliliter improvement in enhanced two. This is not only statistically significant, it's also clinically meaningful. Remember this was on top of lambas and labas as well.  cram.com. We actually have a video on obstructive sleep apnea explained clearly. And today we're going to talk more about this topic. And that's a condition where basically at the back of your throat, your tongue, which is this structure right here, tends to be enlarged and the airway tends to be small and it tends to go back and actually obstruct that. So, while you're trying to take a breath in, you're trying to have air suck down into your airways, it can't because there's an obstruction there at the back of your throat. And when that happens, no air goes down, no oxygen goes down, your brain picks up the fact that your oxygen levels are low and it arouses you out of your sleep and causes you not to get good sleep, causes your heart rate to go up, your adrenaline to go up, and all sorts of cardiovascular complications can occur. Now, each of these events, whether it's an apnea completely stop breathing or a hypopnea where the flow reduces by 30%, this can occur over a 10 second period of time. And typically we see a 3 or 4% desaturation in oxygen saturation during the night. If we see this up to 4.9 times per hour, that is actually considered normal. So, this is known as the apnea hypopnea index. How many times this happens per hour? Again, 0 to 4.9 is considered perfectly normal. However, if it's between 5 and 14.9 events per hour, that is considered mild. And if somebody has symptoms of obstructive sleep apnea, that is a situation we would like to treat it. If there are no symptoms, however, we generally don't treat that unless there's cardiovascular complications that the patient also has like high blood pressure, history of stroke, etc. Certainly people with moderate or severe sleep apnea, so that means people with AHIs of 15 to 29.9 or greater than 30 events per hour, which would be severe, are the type of people that we really want to make sure that we're treating because this type of a condition, obstructive sleep apnea, is associated with the number of bad things, cardiovascular events, neurovascular events, neurological mood, etc. One of the major treatments for obstructive sleep apnea is actually something called a CPAP mask. This either fits over the nose and the mouth, or it fits just over the nose if you can keep the mouth closed, or it fits into the nose again if you can keep the mouth closed. The purpose of this is to put in this positive pressure ventilation, almost as if you're pumping up a flat tire, to get the tongue off of the back of the throat and to leave this open. === FILE: Multicenter Double Blinded RCT on Vit D in Multiple Sclerosis [TRH-xcWzi2E].txt ===

 cram.com. This is a paper that was published on March 10, 2025 in the JAMA Network titled High-Dose Vitamin D in Clinically Isolated Syndrome Typical of Multiple Sclerosis, the D-Lay MS Randomize Clinical Trial. This was a double-blinded randomized control trial and it was multi-centered, basically the gold standard in terms of control trials. And what they did was they recruited people between the ages of 18 and 55 for symptoms of multiple sclerosis. Specifically, they looked for people who had symptoms of multiple sclerosis, they had MRI findings, and they had oligoclonal bands, which are proteins that are found in the spinal fluid of these patients. And the other thing that's important to understand is their vitamin D levels were less than 40 nanograms per milliliter, or another way of saying that is less than 100 nanomoles per liter. So make sure that you understand that these are two different scales. The one that we use typically in the United States is 40 nanograms per milliliter. So as it turns out, they got 316 of these subjects. However, only 303 received any one dose and a total of 288 received all the doses. What exactly was it that they were given? Let's look at this analysis of those that got just at least one dose and what they did was they randomized them to getting 100,000 international units every two weeks times two years versus placebo. Remember, this was a double blinded. That means the patient or the subject did not know if they were getting the drug or not. The person giving them did not know. This was at multiple centers and it was randomized. This gets rid of a lot of confounders. 100,000 international units is pretty high every two weeks for two years. When they did this analysis on the 303, there was 156 in the intervention group and there was 147 in the placebo group. What they did at the end of those two years was they looked at MRI reports to see whether or not there were any of those new lesions that were seen. Here's what they found. There were 94 lesions that were found in the intervention group, which was then about 60.3% of the group. Whereas in the placebo group, there was 109 lesions, which was 74.1%. This difference was statistically significant with a p-value of equal to 0.004. In the intervention group with vitamin D, there was less of these new MRI findings compared to those that got the placebo. However, in this case, the absolute risk reduction is simply the difference between 60.3 and 74.1, and that turns out to be 13.8% absolute risk reduction. In other words, if you gave 100,000 international units to somebody with multiple sclerosis as the subjects in this study were diagnosed with every two weeks for two years, you could reduce the risk of recurrence in these patients or new occurrences by 13.8% overall. There's a number that you can do called the number needed to treat. In other words, how many people would you need to treat for two years with these high doses of vitamin D to prevent one recurrence? It's simply just one divided by the absolute risk reduction, which in this case would be 7.24, but we always round up, which means it would be 8. So we'd treat eight people for two years with high doses of vitamin D. We could prevent one recurrence. So that endpoint was looking at radiographic findings, which may be more sensitive. This is how it's made to be more quote efficient, even though we associate with efficiency, something that's doing the same job, but requiring less energy. Here it's debatable whether or not LEDs are actually doing the same job. In fact, as of late, there's been a number of articles that have been written and scientific studies that have investigated this issue with LED bulbs and their effect on the human body. Part of the issue is how we defined efficiency and how it's defined in the Department of Energy in the United States. The Department of Energy regulates light bulbs and specifically GSLs, which this is one of. It's a general service lamp. And these are the type of bulbs that you would buy at a home improvement store in the terms of lumens per watt. In other words, how bright is the bulb based on the amount of power that's going into the bulb? And as it turns out, lumens are actually a measurement of primarily green and yellow light. And that is concentrated here in the middle of the visible spectrum. All of this other wavelengths that come out of a light emitting device potentially, such as an incandescent bulb or even the sun, is considered to be wasted because it does not contribute to lumens. This measurement of lumens, which was actually standardized in 1924, is the standard that we're currently using to measure efficiency in our light bulb. So watts go in and lumens come out. If you want to improve the efficiency of a bulb, all you need to do is sacrifice some of these wavelengths on the outsides so that you get more efficiency here in the middle. To understand what's happened, we have to look at the history of the energy regulations in the Department of Energy. And for this history, I'd like to thank Dr. Martin Moore Ede for summarizing this and helping with this presentation. In 2007, the Energy Independence and Security Act, which was passed by Congress, established a backstop requirement for general service lamps. These are basically the bulbs that you buy for your home to meet a minimum of 45 lumens per watt. This is an important number because a regular incandescent bulb has an efficiency of only about 10 to 15 lumens per watt because much of that energy goes out into the infrared spectrum. And so to meet that range of 45 lumens per watt, much of that output in the infrared spectrum had to be sacrificed. As it says here, this effectively banned most incandescent and halogen light bulbs that fit normal lamp sockets widely available in homes. After this legislation was passed, there was a number of years that were taken up in the Department of Energy looking at rulemaking so that this could actually be enforced. And in April of 2022, the Department of Energy had the final rules that came out that essentially banned the sale of general service lamps that produced less than 45 lumens per watt. And that effectively banned the sale of new incandescent bulbs after July of 2023. As high as that 45 lumens per watt was, it still allowed bulb makers to manipulate and to move around the requirements so that it could meet the 45 lumens per watt here in the yellow to green area, but still allow some other wavelengths specifically here into the reds and then sometimes even into the blues. And there have been a number of companies that have actually capitalized on that ability. I have no ties to any of the companies that I'm mentioning today. I'm just showing you an example. For instance, there's a company called Chorus, which has made bulbs that, depending on the time of day, can have maximum blue light in the morning, which is beneficial for cortisol production, but minimal in terms of blue light in the evening when you want to minimize the stimulation of retinal ganglion cells that would shut down melatonin production. And you can see there's other types of lights that they can do inside of that visible spectrum. There's also a company called Nira, which, as you can see, takes these LED bulbs, which have a very limited spectrum here in the visible spectrum. And then what it does is it puts a filament into the bulb that produces infrared light across this spectrum, which then is able to simulate more closely that of sunlight spectrum. And these bulbs still make it within the 45 lumens per watt standard. There's also another company called PhosphorTech, which coats the bulb where you can actually use a shade around any type of bulb because it will take the light from the blue spectrum here, use photochemical reactions in the coating to convert it into deep red and even infrared light so that it would simulate a sunset. As a result of this, we've seen an increase, dramatic increase, in the amount of LED bulbs that are now in homes from 4% to 47%. And well, as we've mentioned, things are going to get worse because there's currently a rule that has already been put in place that in July of 2028, there will be an increase once again in the floor of the efficiency standards. This was in April of 2024. The DOE published a new final rule. And you can see the final rule here. I will give you a link to it in the description below. And basically what it does is it raises that 45 lumen per watt minimum standard to now 125 lumens per watt. And it depends on the bulb that we're talking about. But depending on which bulb it is that we're talking about, as you can see here, this is published from the rule. It can be, as it says here, 124.6, 115.7, 96, 92.3, and so forth. And you can see here that these are all well above 45 lumens per watt. And so what that essentially means is that they're going to have to sacrifice even more natural wavelengths that they would normally have in this bulb to squeeze it to get more lumens out of it given the same amount of watts. So the question is, is why would they do that? The reason why they would do that is they want to get more efficiency because they believe that the more efficient light bulbs are, the less energy that has to be produced. And the less energy that has to be produced, the less greenhouse gases will be produced and the less pollution. In fact, in their cost analysis of this, they predicted that there would be money saved. They actually calculated that there would be health benefits. And those health benefits were from less and less particulate matters like PM 2.5, which is the amount of fine particles in the air that can cause disease. Let's look at nighttime. Nighttime control not for breakfast but for dinner. Notice not a huge difference between any of these in terms of glucose and insulin. Let's go back once more. Breakfast is where we saw a big difference. Dinner not that much of a difference. So here's a summary that I put together of what the authors got from this study. This study shows that simulated night shift work causes internal circadian misalignment and impairs glucose tolerance largely due to mistime food intake. When participants ate meals at night, that's in the group on the left, their circadian glucose rhythms shifted out of sync with central circadian rhythms resulting in impaired glucose tolerance and reduced pancreatic B cell function. In contrast, when meals were restricted to the daytime in the group on the right, circadian alignment was preserved despite night work and glucose tolerance remained normal. These findings suggest that food timing strongly influences peripheral circadian rhythms and metabolic regulation independent of the sleep-wake cycle. Eating at night disrupts circadian coordination and glucose metabolism while daytime eating aligns feeding with the central circadian pacemaker, maintaining pancreatic function and preserving dysglycemia. This study highlights that meal timing as a practical behavioral intervention to mitigate the metabolic risk of shift work, particularly the elevated risk of glucose intolerance, insulin resistance, and type 2 diabetes. Since most night workers do not fully adapt their circadian system to the night schedules, aligning food intake to the habitual daytime may be an effective strategy to protect metabolic health. Okay, so we just looked at this first study here. Now what we're going to do is look at the follow-up study that was just published this year in 2025, looking at these secondary analyses in these endpoints. So here's a category looking at something called the PNN50. The group here on the left able to eat any time of day or night, and those on the right were restricted to only eating during the day. And you can see a huge difference here. Black, again, is the baseline, as you can see here. And if you notice in the group that was restricted to the day, there's not a huge difference between those numbers that they got at the beginning and those numbers that they got at the end. However, there's a huge drop in the PNN50. So what does that mean? What is the PNN50? It indicates that being low reduced vagal tone, that means the vagus nerve, which is a parasympathetic nerve, and a relative shift towards sympathetic dominance. What we're seeing here is because the PNN50, which is a measure of heart rate, variability, and things, when that goes down, that means that we're seeing more of a sympathetic nervous system drive. That's not good. So where do we see this? We can see this in stress, anxiety, poor sleep, acute illness, sepsis, systemic inflammation, cardiovascular disease like heart failure, post-MI, diabetes, and other metabolic disorders, and also aging. So clinically, a low PNN50 and a low heart rate variability in general is associated with worse cardiovascular and overall health outcomes, including a higher risk of arrhythmias and mortality, especially in cardiac patients. What are we seeing here after just a week or two, making sure that these subjects are working at night and they're able to eat at any time? We're seeing a pretty dramatic shift in something that we know is predictive of these bad outcomes. Here, in that cohort, those subjects that we're told don't eat at night, only eat during the day, we don't see any of it. We're able to completely mitigate the surrogate for bad outcomes. Let's look for another outcome. This is called the RMSSDMS. And again, we see that it's dropped as well in our group that was given food all throughout the day and night. What is the RMSSD? It's the square root of the mean of the square differences between the NN intervals. This is looking at the heart, primarily reflects parasympathetic activity. So it is going down. And of course, if parasympathetic activity is going down, it indicates that there's more sympathetic activity. So again, if it's low, which it is here in the group that was allowed to eat all throughout the day and night, it indicated reduced vagal tone, seen in stress, illness, aging, and cardiovascular metabolic disease. It's widely used in both clinical cardiology and sports physiology as a marker of recovery and autonomic balance. And see here, it's out of balance. Just within a week or two of doing this. Let's look at another one here, the LF to HF ratio. What is the LF to HF ratio? This looks at sympathetic dominance. And again, it's up in stress, anxiety, illness, and in heart failure. It's common in acute stress states or cardiovascular disease. And what do we see here? It's actually up, just as we would predict. But we do not see it up in the group that refrained from eating food at night, only ate it during the day. This is the plasminogen activator inhibitor one, otherwise known as PAI1. Once again, we see an increase in these patients, but we don't see it in the patients that were restricted to eating during the day. In the context here of blood clotting, an elevation in PAI1 plays a critical role in maintaining hemostatic balance. Only PAI1 levels can impair fibrinolysis, that means the breakdown of blood clots, and this leads to increased thrombosis risk. This is an issue and may explain why we see increased blood clots in those people who work at night. For instance, it has been identified as a major determinant in inhibiting arterial clot lysis and is linked to pathological processes, such as inflammation and cardiovascular events. We have seen multiple endpoints here that show that at least in this cohort, there is a way to mitigate, reduce, almost completely eliminate in some of these cases, the added risk of working at night. There are some limitations to this study. These were people that were 18 to 30 years of age, so this may not work as well for middle age and older adults. This is a small sample size. There was 10 in one group and about 10 in the other group. There was some strict inclusion criteria. It would be nice to see this a little bit more opened up. Here's the other limitation. These participants were not actual shift workers that have been doing it for years. These were people that were brought onto the shift and had their circadian rhythm shifted over and then used those subjects in the trial to do a randomized intervention, which restricted feeding to just during the day. Another limitation was that circadian misalignment was induced by a forced desynchrony protocol, which may not fully reflect real life night shift conditions. Really, this needs to be done again on a larger platform. Laboratory conditions provided high experimental to control, but limit direct translation to real world settings. Unclear in a real world setting if this would have the same type of benefit or maybe the same benefit, but to maybe a less of a degree. Because of all these limitations, we really need to do this type of study and larger clinical trials involving real shift workers, permanent rotating irregular morning and evening shifts to confirm whether or not this type of intervention will have the same benefits in these broader populations. Until that time comes, we can say that apparently one of the things that night shift workers may want to do if they want to get a benefit of this, let's see if it works for them, is to work at night as they are. When they get home, it's to have a meal, to sleep for about three hours, wake up, have a meal, sleep for three hours, wake up, have a meal, and then that last meal may be optional. They want to do that before they go to work. Then when they're at work, the key here is not eating anything. That might be difficult because I know the culture at night. I know that people bring things in for the nurses at night like pizza, donuts, they have parties. It's not easy to not eat. Now, of course, this goes beyond the medical culture because there are night shift workers all over the world doing things more than just medicine. I don't know exactly what their cultures are, but I can assume it's probably very similar because human beings are pretty similar wherever you go. It may be, based on this evidence, if this is applicable to a larger population, that refraining from eating on your night shift may actually prevent you from having many of these terrible diseases down the road like heart disease, diabetes, obesity, strokes, cancer, things of that nature. So I hope you enjoyed this. If you are a night shift worker, you should review this and we'll put the links to those studies in the description below. The nice thing about relative risk reduction is that this relative risk reduction does not change regardless of what the prevalence is in the disease population. If we're talking about coronary artery disease, things that don't change dramatically in terms of prevalence, then I would prefer the absolute risk reduction. It's a little bit more robust.  medcram.com. Okay, well, welcome to another MedCram lecture. We're gonna talk about urinalysis again, and this time we're gonna go into the urinalysis of glucose, ketones, bilirubin, urobulinogen, and specific gravity with respect to the urine. Okay, let's get started with glucose. We use a peroxide method, which will become important later. So we use peroxides to measure glucose, a peroxide reaction. And the thing that you should know that's important is that when blood goes to the nephron, there is a barrier there in the nephron that is preventing glucose from being spilled into Bowman's capsule and the proximal convoluted tubule. So if you can kind of imagine a dam with a wall and there is water, so long as the water doesn't go over that dam wall, there won't be spillage over it. That's the same analogy that we use with glucose. And the number that you should know for glucose is called the TM, which stands for the transport maximum. And in this case, that number is 180 micrograms per deciliter, which means that if the glucose concentration is greater than 180 milligrams per deciliter, then there could be spillage of glucose into the urine. But if it is less than 180 milligrams per deciliter, there should not be. So you should know that there are SGLT2 inhibitors, and these are the medications for diabetes that end in flosin, like impagyliflosin and the pagliflosin. These are medications that actually work by dumping glucose into the urine. And so it can cause glycoceurea, even though there is a normal or elevated blood glucose, but not above the 180 that we talked about. A positive urine glucose on these agents does not necessarily indicate hyperglycemia. As we talked about before, there is a way to make this thing a false negative, and so you have to be careful of that. There's two possibilities using our analogy. Either you've got a bad wall, okay, that means that the dam wall is not holding back the water and it's leaking out, or you've got spillover. Okay, so the bad wall is much more rare and the spillover is much more common. So let's talk about the bad wall first. This is sometimes known as Fanconi's syndrome. With Fanconi's syndrome, you have a problem with reabsorption at the proximal convoluted tubules. So you also see dumping of phosphate. You'll also see dumping of uric acid. You'll also see dumping of bicarbonate. Now, if you remember, if you don't reabsorb bicarbonate well at the proximal convoluted tubule, this is known as a renal tubular acidosis type two. Okay, and that gets into the pH. And then of course the other thing that you see that gets dumped are amino acids. Now, what are things that can cause this Fanconi's syndrome? Well, you could have something called multiple myeloma. That is a cancerous disease where you have plasma cells that overpopulate. Other things that could do this would be heavy metals. Other things that could do this would be medications. For instance, Tenover, which is an HIV medication. Chemotherapeutic agents like cisplatin can do it. So in terms of spillover though, where the wall is fine, but you just have too much glucose in the blood, the biggest one there you gotta know about is diabetes. And anything basically that'll increase your blood glucose, for instance, Cushing's disease, that could do it. And that list goes pretty deep. So those are the causes of glucose in the urine. So what are the major reasons why somebody would have ketones in their urine? One of them is alcohol, so alcoholic ketosis. Another one would be of course, DKA, which is diabetic ketoacidosis. And then finally the last one would be starvation. Ketoacidosis. So what are ketones specifically? Well, the thing that you've gotta remember is in the cell, you've got the nucleus of course, but you've got these mitochondria all throughout. And in the mitochondria, inside them specifically, are where fatty acids get transported into the matrix of the mitochondria, where something called beta oxidation occurs. And beta oxidation takes fatty acids and transforms them into acetyl-CoA, which are two carbon units. So these two carbon units, if there's a lot of this fatty acid transformation into two carbon units, a lot of acetyl-CoA becomes available. And normally that acetyl-CoA, as you already know, should go into the Krebs cycle. But if there's too much of it around and the Krebs cycle can't handle it, then they start to coalesce with each other. And so if you have this acetyl-CoA, which looks somewhat like this, and you bind these together, you're gonna get things like acetoacetate and beta-hydroxybutyrate and acetone. If you have two of these, you're going to make one acetoacetate. And what does acetoacetate look like? It looks like this. And there's one, two, three, four carbons. Now, acetoacetate can be converted and goes back and forth to beta-hydroxybutyrate to look like this. And as you can see here, we have a carbonyl group being reduced to a hydroxy group. And so because this is being reduced, something has to be oxidized, and that's where you take NADH, and you oxidize it to NAD+. So you can get beta-hydroxybutyrate, you can get acetoacetate. Of course, what you can do is you could simply just chop off the CO2, and then what you would get then is simply acetone, which you breathe off. So when you do the nitro-pressite test, it checks for specifically acetoacetate.
